● Created /home/senthilkumarl/mavsdk_drone_show/CLAUDE.md.

  Key things it captures that aren't obvious from casually reading the tree:

  - Two parallel swarm stacks coexist (coordinator.py + src/ vs.
  mavlink_swarm_controller.py) — they don't share state and shouldn't be unified
   or run simultaneously against the same drone.
  - The mission-code table (0/1/2/10+/100/101/102) has to stay consistent across
   Params.Mission, DroneSetup.schedule_mission, and
  DroneCommunicator.process_packet.
  - Fleet identity convention: {hw_id}.hwID marker file in cwd + derived ports
  (50040+hw_id gRPC, 14550+hw_id MAVLink, 13540+hw_id debug).
  - Dashboard port map (3000 React / 5000 Flask GCS / 5001 elevation proxy) and
  the endpoints the React app hits.
  - Gotchas: mavsdk_server_local runs a mock, not the real binary; many .pyc
  files are tracked in git; coordinator.py uses sudo date -s for time sync.

  I kept generic advice ("write tests", "don't leak secrets") out and stuck to
  the project-specific knowledge that takes multiple files to piece together.

❯ i want to create a independent python script which uses the leader follower

  Read 3 files (ctrl+o to expand)

● Before I write it — two quick questions so I don't build the wrong thing:

  1. What does "optimal" mean here? Two common interpretations, very different
  scripts:
    - (a) Geometric / kinematic — heading-rotated formation offsets at each
  leader sample, with velocity/acceleration from finite differences and yaw
  auto-filled from path tangent. Fast, deterministic, ~100 lines. Reuses the
  existing swarm_trajectory_calculator.py.
    - (b) True optimization — minimum-jerk or minimum-snap polynomials per
  segment, with velocity/acceleration limits per drone. Needs scipy.optimize or
  a QP solver (e.g. cvxpy). ~300 lines, slower, but gives you actually-optimal
  smooth trajectories.
  2. Output format — do you want the per-drone CSV schema that the existing
  offboard_from_csv.py consumes
  (idx,t,px,py,pz,vx,vy,vz,ax,ay,az,yaw,mode,ledr,ledg,ledb, NED with pz = 
  -altitude), or the simpler t,north,east,altitude,yaw that matches the leader
  path format?

  My default guess is (a) + existing offboard CSV schema, written as a new file
  generate_swarm_trajectories.py at the repo root, CLI: --config 
  drones_config.json --formation spear_formation_swarm.csv --leader-path 
  leader_path_with_heading.csv --output-dir trajectories_output/. Say "go" and
  I'll build that, or redirect me.

  Read 1 file, listed 1 directory (ctrl+o to expand)

● Done. New independent folder Simple_swarm/:

  Simple_swarm/
  ├── generate_trajectories.py   # leader/follower trajectory generator
  ├── visualize_trajectories.py  # 3D + top-down + altitude + min-separation
  plots
  └── output/                    # per-drone CSVs + swarm_trajectories.png

  Commands (from inside Simple_swarm/):

  # uses repo-root sample files by default
  python3 generate_trajectories.py
  python3 generate_trajectories.py --plot          # generate + open the plot

  # explicit inputs
  python3 generate_trajectories.py \
    --config ../drones_config.json \
    --formation ../spear_formation_swarm.csv \
    --leader-path ../leader_path_with_heading.csv \
    --output-dir output

  # visualize whatever is already in output/
  python3 visualize_trajectories.py --no-show --save-dir output

  What it does

  - Reads drones_config.json to find the leader (is_leader: true), the formation
   CSV for offsets, and the leader path CSV.
  - For each timestep: rotates every follower's (offset_n, offset_e) by the
  leader's heading and adds the altitude offset. If the leader path has no
  heading column, heading is auto-derived from the path tangent.
  - Computes vx/vy/vz and ax/ay/az via np.gradient over the actual t column
  (handles non-uniform sampling correctly — the existing
  swarm_trajectory_calculator.py assumes a fixed dt, which this fixes).
  - Writes one CSV per drone in the schema offboard_from_csv.py consumes:
  idx,t,px,py,pz,vx,vy,vz,ax,ay,az,yaw,mode,ledr,ledg,ledb (NED, pz = -altitude,
   yaw in radians, mode=70, leader in red LED).
  - Visualization renders 3D paths, top-down view, altitude-vs-time, and the
  minimum pairwise distance (safety check — flagged against a 1m dashed
  reference).

  Notes / tradeoffs

  - This is the geometric/kinematic variant I described as option (a). If you
  want true optimization (min-snap polynomials with velocity/acceleration limits
   per drone), that's a different pass — say the word and I'll add it as a
  --smooth=minjerk option using scipy.
  - Self-contained: no imports from coordinator.py, src/, or
  swarm_trajectory_calculator.py.
  - Requires numpy, pandas, matplotlib (already in the repo's environment).

      107 +def motion_tangent_yaw(vx, vy, fallback_rad):                 
      108 +    """Per-sample yaw from horizontal velocity, unwrapped, wit
          +h low-speed fallback."""                                      
      109 +    speed = np.hypot(vx, vy)                                  
      110 +    yaw = np.arctan2(vy, vx)                                  
      111 +    low = speed < YAW_MOTION_SPEED_FLOOR                      
      112 +    yaw = np.where(low, fallback_rad, yaw)                    
      113 +    return np.unwrap(yaw)

● The yaw columns now differ meaningfully:

  - Leader t=0: yaw=0.0 (commanded heading)
  - Follower 2 t=0: yaw=0.574 rad ≈ 33° (motion-tangent: atan2(1.59, 2.45))
  - Leader t=1: yaw=0.262 rad = 15° (commanded)
  - Follower 2 t=1: yaw=0.624 rad ≈ 36° (tracks its own velocity)

  Summary of the change:

  generate_trajectories.py
  - New motion_tangent_yaw(): computes per-sample yaw from atan2(vy, vx),
  unwraps to avoid 2π jumps, falls back to leader heading when horizontal speed
  < 0.1 m/s (avoids garbage yaw during near-hover).
  - build_trajectory_df(..., yaw_mode=...) with two modes:
    - "motion" — each drone's yaw tracks its own direction of travel (default 
  for followers).
    - "leader" — copies leader heading (used for the leader itself; available
  for followers via --follower-yaw leader if you want the old behavior).
  - New CLI flag: --follower-yaw {motion, leader} (default motion).

  visualize_trajectories.py
  - Top-down view now draws heading arrows at 8 evenly-spaced points along each
  drone's path (via ax.quiver). Leader arrows in red, followers in grey. The
  arrow direction is computed directly from the CSV's yaw column, so it reflects
   whatever yaw was written.

  Run it with:
  python3 generate_trajectories.py                       # motion-tangent yaw 
  (new default)
  python3 generate_trajectories.py --follower-yaw leader # old behavior
  python3 visualize_trajectories.py --no-show --save-dir output

✻ Brewed for 2m 3s

❯ the trjectories of follower should also based on leader heading               

● To make sure I don't keep flipping this, let me restate what the code does and
   what I'm changing so you can correct me:

  What's already happening (was from the first version, unchanged):
  - Follower positions are rotated by the leader's heading at each timestep —
  formation rotates in real time as the leader turns.
  follower_positions_over_time multiplies the offset by a rotation matrix built
  from leader_df["heading"] per-sample.

  What I'm changing now: flip the default follower yaw back to "track leader
  heading." Each follower's yaw column will match the leader's heading at that
  timestep, so all drones face the same direction as the leader (rigid formation
   look). The motion-tangent option stays available via a flag.

   the leader's heading-0 frame; at t=3 it has swung to (10.83, 4.0) as the
  leader heading reached 45°).

  Summary

  - Default --follower-yaw is now leader (follower yaw = leader heading
  per-sample).
  - Positions unchanged — they've always been rotated by leader heading in real
  time via follower_positions_over_time.
  - To switch individual-drone real-time motion yaw back on, run python3 
  generate_trajectories.py --follower-yaw motion.

● Animation saved (Simple_swarm/output/swarm_animation.gif, 381 KB, 1000×800).

  New file: Simple_swarm/animate_trajectories.py

  What each frame shows
  - Red marker = leader; colored markers = followers.
  - Thin fading trail behind each drone (last --trail-length samples, default
  25).
  - Grey polygon connecting all drones in the current frame — this is what
  you'll see rotating as the leader's heading sweeps through 360°.
  - Short arrow out of each marker = that drone's yaw direction.
  - Title updates every frame with t and the leader's current heading in
  degrees.

  Commands (from Simple_swarm/)
  python3 animate_trajectories.py                       # writes 
  output/swarm_animation.gif
  python3 animate_trajectories.py --fps 20 --trail-length 40
  python3 animate_trajectories.py --show                # also open an 
  interactive window
  python3 animate_trajectories.py --output custom.gif --max-frames 200

  Notes / tradeoffs
  - GIF output uses matplotlib's PillowWriter — no ffmpeg dependency, works on
  any install that already has Pillow. If you want a crisper MP4, we can swap to
   FFMpegWriter behind a --format flag.
  - --max-frames downsamples long trajectories (default cap 120 frames) to keep
  the GIF small; increase it for longer paths.
  - 3D quivers can't be updated in place in matplotlib, so the script recreates
  heading arrows each frame. That costs a little render time but keeps the code
  simple.

  Now the Simple_swarm/ folder has: generate_trajectories.py (planner) →
  visualize_trajectories.py (static 4-panel plot) → animate_trajectories.py (3D
  GIF animation).

❯ i want a light weight simulator to test our swarm. any ideas?                 

● Two realistic tiers, depending on what you want to catch:

  1. Kinematic playback simulator (lightest, ~200 lines of pure Python in
  Simple_swarm/). Replay the generated CSVs as setpoints into a per-drone
  double-integrator with velocity/acceleration/yaw-rate limits and some tracking
   lag, then plot commanded vs achieved. Catches infeasible segments (too-fast
  turns, altitude climb saturation), formation breaks, and ground collisions —
  but it's pure kinematics, no aerodynamics or PX4 in the loop.
  2. Headless PX4 SITL (medium). Launch N copies of px4 none_iris — same binary
  as multiple_sitl.sh but without gzserver/gzclient — and point MAVSDK at them
  on ports 14540+i. Real autopilot + MAVSDK in the loop, actually exercises your
   mavlink_swarm_controller.py path. Costs ~100 MB RAM per drone and requires
  the PX4 build that's already under multiple_sitl/PX4-Autopilot/.

  My recommendation: start with (1) — it's the fastest way to validate that the
  planner's output is physically sane before you ever talk to an autopilot,
  reuses the CSVs and visualize_trajectories.py you already have, and takes ~1
  file to write. Step up to (2) only when you want to validate the
  commanding/timing glue. Want me to build (1)?

      output/simulated_tight > /dev/null 2>&1; echo "exit=$?")
  ⎿  exit=1

● Exit code 1 as expected — the earlier 0 was just tail's exit, not the sim's.
  CI-usable.

  What Simple_swarm/simulate_swarm.py does

  Reads the commanded CSVs from generate_trajectories.py, upsamples each to a 50
   Hz integrator grid, and runs a double-integrator PD tracker per drone:

  acc_cmd = kp*(pos_set - pos) + kv*(vel_set - vel)     # PD
  acc = saturate(acc_cmd, max_accel_{xy,z})
  vel += acc·dt;  vel = saturate(vel, max_speed_{xy,up,down})
  pos += vel·dt
  yaw_rate_cmd = kyaw*(yaw_set - yaw);  yaw_rate = saturate(±max_yaw_rate);  yaw
   += yaw_rate·dt

  Horizontal and vertical axes saturate independently. NED sign conventions are
  respected (negative vz = climb).

  Output
  - output/simulated/drone_N[_leader].csv — achieved trajectories in the same 
  schema as the commanded CSVs, so visualize_trajectories.py --trajectories-dir 
  output/simulated and animate_trajectories.py --trajectories-dir 
  output/simulated work unchanged.
  - Console report: per-drone peak + RMS tracking error, saturation percentages
  (accel / vel / yaw-rate), global min pairwise separation, min altitude.
  - commanded_vs_simulated.png when --plot is set (3D overlay + tracking error
  vs time).
  - Exit code 1 if any threshold is crossed.

  Tunables
  Pass a JSON file with any subset of these to override defaults:
  {
    "max_speed_xy": 10.0, "max_speed_up": 5.0, "max_speed_down": 3.0,
    "max_accel_xy": 5.0, "max_accel_z": 3.0,
    "max_yaw_rate": 1.5708,
    "kp_pos": 4.0, "kv_vel": 3.0, "kyaw": 4.0,
    "sim_dt": 0.02
  }

  Commands
  python3 simulate_swarm.py                                   # default limits, 
  writes output/simulated/
  python3 simulate_swarm.py --plot                            # + 
  commanded_vs_simulated.png
  python3 simulate_swarm.py --limits my_drone.json            # custom limits
  python3 simulate_swarm.py --output-dir output/simulated_tight
  # Chain with existing visualizer / animator:
  python3 visualize_trajectories.py --trajectories-dir output/simulated
  --no-show --save-dir output/simulated
  python3 animate_trajectories.py  --trajectories-dir output/simulated --output
  output/simulated/achieved.gif

  What it catches, what it doesn't
  - Catches: infeasible speeds/accelerations, over-aggressive yaw slew,
  inter-drone collisions, ground collisions, PD tracking lag on sharp commands.
  - Doesn't catch: wind/gusts, aerodynamic effects (propwash, blade flapping),
  battery sag, PX4 mode-transition timing, MAVLink latency/loss. For those you
  need tier (2) — headless PX4 SITL.

  Current Simple_swarm/ folder:
  generate_trajectories.py    # plans commanded trajectories
  visualize_trajectories.py   # static 4-panel plot
  animate_trajectories.py     # 3D GIF animation
  simulate_swarm.py           # kinematic playback simulator  ← new
  output/                     # commanded CSVs + plots + GIF
  output/simulated/           # achieved CSVs + overlay plot

      294 +        idx = int(frames[fi])                                 
      295 +        poly_x, poly_y, poly_z = [], [], []                   
      296 +        max_err = 0.0                                         
      297 +                                                              
      298 +        for i in ids:                                         
      299 +            sim = sim_by_id[i]                                
      300 +            cmd = cmd_by_id[i]                                
      301 +                                                              
      302 +            sx = sim["px"].to_numpy(); sy = sim["py"].to_numpy
          +(); sz = -sim["pz"].to_numpy()                                
      303 +            cx = cmd["px"].to_numpy(); cy = cmd["py"].to_numpy
          +(); cz = -cmd["pz"].to_numpy()                                
      304 +                                                              
      305 +            start = max(0, idx - trail_length)                
      306 +            sim_trails[i].set_data_3d(sx[start:idx + 1], sy[st
          +art:idx + 1], sz[start:idx + 1])                              
      307 +            sim_markers[i].set_data_3d([sx[idx]], [sy[idx]], [
          +sz[idx]])                                                     
      308 +            cmd_ghosts[i].set_data_3d([cx[idx]], [cy[idx]], [c
          +z[idx]])                                                      
      309 +            err_lines[i].set_data_3d([sx[idx], cx[idx]], [sy[i
          +dx], cy[idx]], [sz[idx], cz[idx]])                            
      310 +                                                              
      311 +            err = float(np.sqrt((sx[idx] - cx[idx]) ** 2 +    
      312 +                                (sy[idx] - cy[idx]) ** 2 +    
      313 +                                (sz[idx] - cz[idx]) ** 2))    
      314 +            if err > max_err:                                 
      315 +                max_err = err                                 
      316 +                                                              
      317 +            poly_x.append(sx[idx]); poly_y.append(sy[idx]); po
          +ly_z.append(sz[idx])                                          
      318 +                                                              
      319 +        if poly_x:                                            
      320 +            poly_x.append(poly_x[0]); poly_y.append(poly_y[0])
          +; poly_z.append(poly_z[0])                                    
      321 +            formation_line.set_data_3d(poly_x, poly_y, poly_z)
      322 +                                                              
      323 +        t_now = sim_by_id[ids[0]]["t"].iloc[idx]              
      324 +        title.set_text(f"t = {t_now:6.2f}s    max tracking err
          +or = {max_err:.2f} m")                                        
      325 +        return [title]                                        
      326 +                                                              
      327 +    anim = FuncAnimation(fig, update, frames=len(frames),     
      328 +                         interval=1000 / fps, blit=False)     
      329 +    save_path.parent.mkdir(parents=True, exist_ok=True)       
      330 +    anim.save(save_path, writer=PillowWriter(fps=fps))        
      331 +    plt.close(fig)                                            
      332 +    print(f"[ok] Saved overlay animation to {save_path}")     
      333 +                                                              
      334 +                                                              
      335  def plot_overlay(commanded, simulated, save_path: Path):
      336      import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
      337      from mpl_toolkits.mplot3d import Axes3D  # noqa: F401

  What you'll see in the animation
  - Solid filled markers = achieved positions (what the PD tracker actually
  flew).
  - Hollow markers = commanded positions (where the planner told them to be at
  this t).
  - Dotted line between each solid/hollow pair = instantaneous tracking error
  vector. It stretches during sharp commanded turns and snaps back once the
  controller catches up.
  - Grey polygon = formation connecting the achieved positions of all drones.
  - Title updates every frame with current t and the worst tracking error across
   the swarm.

  Test procedure
  # One-time setup (regenerates test_inputs/ deterministically from 
  _gen_test_inputs.py)
  python3 _gen_test_inputs.py

  # Full run
  python3 run_tests.py

  # Filter + verbose
  python3 run_tests.py --filter spiral -v

  # CI hook: non-zero exit on any failure
  python3 run_tests.py && echo "all green"

  How expectations work
  Each scenario has a meta.json:
  {
    "name": "01_basic_spear",
    "expect_simulation": "ok",          // or "fail" for intentionally 
  infeasible
    "expect_peak_err_max": 1.5,         // optional: max allowable peak tracking
   error (m)
    "expect_min_sep_min": 1.0,          // optional: min allowable pairwise 
  separation (m)
    "follower_yaw": "leader"            // optional: pass-through to generator
  }
  The runner parses simulate_swarm.py's stdout report for the actual peak error
  / min separation and compares against these bounds. You can tighten the bounds
   in meta.json to catch regressions (I verified by setting expect_peak_err_max:
   0.01 — the runner correctly failed the scenario and exited 1).

  Adding a new scenario
  Add a scenario_NN_foo(root) function in _gen_test_inputs.py, call it from
  main(), run python3 _gen_test_inputs.py && python3 run_tests.py.

❯ how swarm trejectaries calculated                                             

● Step by step, what generate_trajectories.py does per timestep to turn a leader
   path + formation into per-drone trajectories:

  1. Load and sanity-check the inputs

  - load_drones_config() parses drones_config.json, finds the single is_leader: 
  true entry.
  - load_formation() filters formation.csv to rows where follow == leader_hw_id
  — the follower list. Each row contributes (offset_n, offset_e, offset_alt) in
  the leader's body frame.
  - load_leader_path() reads leader_path.csv, sorts by t.

  2. Fill in the leader's heading if it isn't given

  If the heading column is missing, it's derived from the path tangent:
  dN = np.gradient(north, t)
  dE = np.gradient(east,  t)
  heading_deg = degrees(atan2(dE, dN))
  So the leader points in the direction it's travelling (0° = north, +90° =
  east).

  3. Place each follower in world NED via rigid-body rotation

  This is the core of the formation math. At every single timestep, each
  follower's body-frame offset (oₙ, o_e) is rotated by the leader's heading
  θ(t), then added to the leader's world position:

  [rotₙ(t)]   [cos θ(t)   -sin θ(t)] [oₙ]
  [rot_e(t)] = [sin θ(t)    cos θ(t)] [o_e]

  followerₙ(t)   = leaderₙ(t)   + rotₙ(t)
  follower_e(t)  = leader_e(t)  + rot_e(t)
  follower_alt(t)= leader_alt(t)+ o_alt        (altitude offset is a plain add,
  no rotation)

  Implemented as vectorised numpy in follower_positions_over_time
  (generate_trajectories.py:83-92). Because θ(t) is per-sample, the whole
  formation rotates around the leader in real time as the leader turns.

  Concrete check with follower 2 (offset = (+2, -2)) tracked through a leader
  heading sweep:

  ┌────────────────┬───────────────────────────────────┐
  │ leader heading │ F2 world-frame offset from leader │
  ├────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────┤
  │ 0°             │ (+2, −2)                          │
  ├────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────┤
  │ 90°            │ (+2, +2)                          │
  ├────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────┤
  │ 180°           │ (−2, +2)                          │
  ├────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────┤
  │ 270°           │ (−2, −2)                          │
  └────────────────┴───────────────────────────────────┘

  Same rigid shape, rotated. Verified against the generated CSVs earlier.

  4. Derive velocity and acceleration from position

  Using the actual t column (handles non-uniform sampling):
  vx = ∇ₜ(north)      ax = ∇ₜ(vx)
  vy = ∇ₜ(east)       ay = ∇ₜ(vy)
  vz = −∇ₜ(altitude)  az = ∇ₜ(vz)
  The vertical sign flip turns "up = positive altitude" into NED where down is
  positive (matches the pz = −altitude convention used by offboard_from_csv.py).
   np.gradient uses centred differences in the interior and forward/backward at
  the endpoints.

  5. Pick each drone's yaw

  Two modes, set via --follower-yaw:

  - leader (default): every follower copies the leader's heading at each
  timestep — rigid formation, everyone faces the same way.
  yaw(t) = radians(heading_leader(t))
  - motion: each follower points along its own velocity vector — "faces where
  it's going."
  yaw(t) = atan2(vy(t), vx(t))                      if ||v_xy(t)|| ≥ 0.1 m/s
         = heading_leader(t)                        otherwise  (avoids garbage
  yaw at near-hover)
  yaw    = np.unwrap(yaw)                           (no 2π jumps)

  The leader itself always uses its commanded heading from the CSV — we don't
  override a deliberately-chosen path yaw with a motion estimate.

  6. Write the output CSV

  One file per drone, schema matching offboard_from_csv.py:
  idx, t, px, py, pz, vx, vy, vz, ax, ay, az, yaw, mode, ledr, ledg, ledb
  where px = north, py = east, pz = -altitude, yaw in radians, mode = 70
  (maneuvering), leader flagged with red LED.

  Recap

  The whole computation is per-timestep, stateless: every row of every
  follower's output is a function of only the leader's state at that same t plus
   the follower's static body-frame offset. There's no inter-drone optimization,
   no smoothing filter, no feedback from followers back to the leader. That's
  why it's called the "geometric/kinematic" variant — if you want true min-snap
  optimization with per-drone velocity/acceleration constraints that would stay
  within flight envelope, that's a different algorithm (scipy QP over polynomial
   coefficients). The current simulator (simulate_swarm.py) is the other half of
   the story: it takes these geometrically-perfect setpoints and shows what a
  drone with real velocity/accel/yaw-rate limits would actually fly.

✻ Sautéed for 36s

❯ do we use kalman filter here                                                  

  Read 1 file (ctrl+o to expand)

● No, Simple_swarm/ does not use a Kalman filter. The trajectory calculation is
  deterministic, stateless, per-timestep algebra — no state estimation, no noise
   model, no measurement fusion. The simulator is a plain PD controller with
  saturation on top of an Euler integrator; there's no estimator in either
  direction.

  However, the older onboard stack elsewhere in this repo does use one — worth
  knowing because they often get confused:

  Kalman filter in the coordinator stack (NOT in Simple_swarm)

  Lives in src/filter.py:4 as a KalmanFilter class. It's a standard linear KF:

  - State (9-dim): position, velocity, acceleration per NED axis:
  [n, vn, an,  e, ve, ae,  d, vd, ad]
  - Transition matrix A: identity (see src/filter.py:35-45). So the "prediction"
   step literally just returns the previous state unchanged — no kinematic model
   baked in. This is unusual; a typical constant-acceleration KF would have Δt
  terms coupling position/velocity/acceleration.
  - Observation matrix H: identity. Every state is directly "observed" by the
  incoming setpoint.
  - Covariances Q, R: both initialised to identity in initialize_if_needed
  (src/filter.py:72-74) — not tuned.

  Where it gets used

  src/drone_config.py:calculate_setpoints (about line 163) applies it to smooth
  the follow setpoint before the offboard controller pushes it to PX4:

  1. Compute raw follow setpoint (LLA → NED via navpy).
  2. kalman_filter.predict() on the 9-state.
  3. Pack the raw setpoint + last predicted acceleration as the "measurement."
  4. kalman_filter.update(measurement) — this is what actually smooths.
  5. OffboardController.maintain_setpoints (src/offboard_controller.py:101)
  pulls kalman_filter.get_current_state() and sends that to MAVSDK.

  So it's a setpoint smoother on the follower — not a state estimator for the
  leader, not an inter-drone fusion. Equivalent role to the "smoothing factor"
  in the mavlink_swarm_controller.py's
  calculate_follower_positions_with_current, just implemented as a KF rather
  than a blend weight.

  Why Simple_swarm skips it

  The KF in src/filter.py exists because the onboard stack runs online at ~4 Hz
  (params.follow_setpoint_frequency), with real-world noise sources: GPS jitter
  on the leader's position, network jitter on the telemetry UDP link, numerical
  noise from the LLA→NED conversion per tick. Smoothing avoids jittery offboard
  setpoints.

  Simple_swarm is offline, deterministic, and operates on exact inputs. There's
  nothing noisy to filter:
  - The leader path is a clean CSV, not a sampled stream.
  - Follower positions are computed directly from the leader path by rotation —
  no estimator needed.
  - Velocity/acceleration come from np.gradient over the whole series at once,
  so there's no forward-only causal smoothing to worry about.

  Adding a KF there would just low-pass the already-clean trajectories,
  introducing lag without improving anything. If you wanted to soften sharp
  corners in the commanded path, the right tool would be a
  minimum-jerk/minimum-snap polynomial fit (the tier-2 "true optimization"
  option I mentioned earlier), not a KF.

  When you'd want to add one to Simple_swarm

  Only if you change the input contract:
  - If the leader path became a noisy recorded telemetry log instead of a clean
  plan, you'd want a KF-backed smoother on the leader before computing
  followers.
  - If the simulator grew measurement noise on the drone's state (to emulate a
  real onboard estimator), each simulated drone would need its own KF to recover
   smooth state from noisy measurements — closer to SITL.

  Neither is the case today.

✻ Churned for 37s

❯ i want to implement this in real time where the leader position is            
  periodically updated to every follower. follower has to maintain the          
  formation based on the leader position and heading                            

● Good direction — this is essentially the pattern the older src/ /
  coordinator.py stack already implements (leader broadcasts telemetry, each
  follower computes its own setpoint via the same rotation math, smoothed by the
   Kalman filter in src/filter.py). Worth understanding that before we pick an
  approach, because some of it may be reusable.

  A couple of architecture choices have very different scope — let me lay them
  out so you can pick:

  Option A — Distributed, each drone runs its own process. One
  realtime_leader.py running on the leader broadcasts (t, n, e, alt, heading, 
  vn, ve, vd) over UDP (unicast per follower or multicast) at 4–10 Hz. Each
  follower runs realtime_follower.py that listens, applies the same
  rotation-by-heading math as generate_trajectories.py, and emits an offboard
  setpoint to its local mavsdk_server. Matches how PX4 companion-computer setups
   actually deploy. Needs jitter/latency handling (a linear extrapolator or
  small KF to bridge dropped packets).

  Option B — Centralized, one ground station. A single realtime_swarm.py on the
  GCS subscribes to the leader's MAVSDK telemetry, computes every follower's
  setpoint in one place, and pushes setpoints to each follower's MAVSDK
  connection. Simpler code, one clock, no packet broadcast — but the GCS becomes
   a single point of failure and link bandwidth scales with drone count. This is
   what mavlink_swarm_controller.py already does.

  Option C — Simulated-only test harness. Same distributed code as (A) but with
  a fake leader process that replays a leader_path.csv in wall-clock time and
  fake followers that run the kinematic simulator in a loop — no PX4, no MAVSDK,
   no network except loopback UDP. Best for validating the protocol/timing
  before touching real hardware.

  My recommendation: build (C) first as a new set of scripts in Simple_swarm/
  (say realtime_leader.py + realtime_follower.py + run_realtime_demo.py), then
  swap in MAVSDK later once the protocol, rate, and smoothing are right. That
  way each piece is independently testable and you don't have to have drones
  connected to iterate. Which option do you want, and is running this on a
  single machine (loopback UDP) or across real networked machines from day one?

✻ Cooked for 35s

  New files in Simple_swarm/ (Option C complete)

  realtime_protocol.py       # shared UDP/JSON schema + port conventions
  realtime_leader.py         # wall-clock leader: reads CSV, broadcasts at
  --rate Hz
  realtime_follower.py       # listens, rotates offset, PD-tracks its own state
  run_realtime_demo.py       # orchestrates leader + N followers as subprocesses

  How it runs

  ┌─── realtime_leader.py ────┐
  │ loads leader_path.csv     │   UDP/JSON {t, n,e,alt, heading, vn,ve,vd}
  │ interpolates to wall clock│──────────────────┐ unicast 127.0.0.1:37540+hw_id
  │ unicasts at --rate        │                  │
  │ logs drone_1_leader.csv   │                  ▼
  └───────────────────────────┘   ┌─── realtime_follower.py (one per drone) ─┐
                                  │ binds 37540+hw_id (non-blocking UDP)      │
                                  │ every sim_dt (20 ms at 50 Hz):            │
                                  │   drain socket → latest packet            │
                                  │   extrapolate leader to now: p + v·Δt     │
                                  │   target = leader + R(heading)·offset_body│
                                  │   PD control + saturation (same as sim)   │
                                  │   log row                                 │
                                  │ exit on silence_timeout                   │
                                  │ writes drone_N.csv                        │
                                  └───────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Every process is its own OS process — when we move to Option B, the only
  change is replacing the inner PD loop with MAVSDK offboard calls to a real
  autopilot.

  python3 realtime_leader.py   --config ... --formation ... --leader-path ...
  --rate 10 --output /tmp/l.csv

  Protocol (wire-level)

  One UDP JSON datagram per leader tick, destined to each follower's port:
  {"seq": 42, "hw_id": 1, "t_leader": 4.20,
   "n": 8.4, "e": 1.8, "alt": 10.84, "heading_deg": 21.0,
   "vn": 2.0, "ve": 0.42, "vd": -0.2}
  Ports: follower hw_id N listens on 127.0.0.1:37540 + N. Port base is
  centralized in realtime_protocol.REALTIME_PORT_BASE and deliberately outside
  the MAVSDK (14540+) and GCS (34550) ranges used elsewhere in the repo, so no
  clashes if you run this alongside coordinator.py stuff.

  Design choices worth knowing before Option B

  - Linear extrapolation between leader packets. If a packet drops, the follower
   advances the leader estimate by last_velocity · Δt_since_last_packet for
  position; heading is frozen (no angular extrapolation). Good enough at 10 Hz
  with localhost, will need a 2nd-order predictor or small KF when you get onto
  a real link.
  - Start synchronization. Followers wait for the first leader packet before
  initializing state (first_packet_timeout default 15 s). That way the initial
  position matches the leader's first commanded position + offset, so there's no
   step error on t=0.
  - Shutdown. Followers exit when the leader has been silent for silence_timeout
   (default 2 s). In real flight you'd replace this with a "hover in place"
  safety behavior; easy knob to flip.
  - Per-drone limits are the simulator defaults. Copy-pasted from
  simulate_swarm.py's DEFAULT_LIMITS. For Option B we'll drop the saturator
  entirely (PX4 does that onboard) and replace the PD integrator with
  drone.offboard.set_position_velocity_ned(...) calls.

  Ready for Option B (MAVSDK real flight) when you are — the swap is mostly in
  realtime_follower.py where the integration loop becomes an async MAVSDK
  offboard loop and the sim state becomes telemetry from
  drone.telemetry.position_velocity_ned().

● Three of four pass; 05_hover_rotate fails with 1.6 m peak error. This is a
  real bug worth fixing before Option B — the test procedure just earned its
  keep.

  Root cause: when the leader hovers and rotates, its linear velocity is zero
  but each follower still has to sweep a 4 m-radius arc at 2.1 m/s. The
  follower's PD target velocity is currently copied directly from the leader's
  velocity (which is 0), so the controller has no feed-forward for the
  rotational component — it catches up through position error only, producing
  steady-state lag ≈ v/kp ≈ 0.53 m plus startup transients.

  The offline planner didn't hit this because np.gradient on the commanded
  position already baked the tangential velocity in. In realtime we need to
  reconstruct it from the leader's yaw rate.

  Fix: extend the wire protocol to carry heading_rate_dps, and have the follower
   add the rotational velocity term to its target:

  ω = radians(heading_rate_dps)
  rot_n, rot_e = R(heading)·(offset_n, offset_e)         # already doing this
  target_vel_rot = (−ω·rot_e,  ω·rot_n,  0)              # new: d/dt of the
  rotated offset
  target_vel = leader_vel + target_vel_rot               # use this for the PD
  feed-forward

      54      df["ve"] =  np.gradient(df["east"].to_numpy(),      t)
      55      df["vd"] = -np.gradient(df["altitude"].to_numpy(),  t)
      56 +    df["heading_rate_dps"] = np.gradient(df["heading_unwrapped"
         +].to_numpy(), t)                                               
      57      return df
      58  
      59

  Summary of the test pass

  - Loop timing is solid: both leader (10 Hz target) and followers (50 Hz
  target) have p95 jitter ≤ 0.3 ms on loopback. Even the worst tick was sub-ms.
  Plenty of headroom to add MAVSDK calls in Option B.
  - Packet counts match expectations exactly: 150 / 200 / 200 / 120 packets for
  15 / 20 / 20 / 12 s paths at 10 Hz.
  - Tracking error is well below the offline planner's own PD tracking error,
  now that rotational-velocity feed-forward is wired up. The hover-rotate
  scenario went from failing to best-in-class.
  - No regressions: the five offline scenarios in run_tests.py still pass
  identically.

  New artifact

  run_realtime_tests.py — iterates the applicable scenarios, spawns the full
  orchestrator, and validates:
  1. Orchestrator + all subprocesses exit cleanly
  2. Leader packet count is within ±10% of rate × duration
  3. Leader tick p95 jitter < 30 ms
  4. Worst-follower tick p95 jitter < 30 ms
  5. Per-follower peak 3D tracking error vs offline ground truth < per-scenario
  tolerance (values in TRACKING_TOL_M)

  Non-zero exit code if any check fails, so it's CI-ready.

  Command
  python3 run_realtime_tests.py                    # full 83 s wall-clock run
  python3 run_realtime_tests.py --filter hover     # single scenario

  Bug caught and fixed

  The test procedure surfaced a real bug: when the leader was rotating in place
  (hover + yaw), followers had no feed-forward for the tangential velocity their
   offset required, producing >1.5 m lag. Fix applied in three files:

  - realtime_protocol.py — added heading_rate_dps to the packet schema
  (backwards-compatible: dict.get(..., 0.0) in the follower).
  - realtime_leader.py — computes heading_rate_dps = 
  np.gradient(heading_unwrapped, t) once at load time, interpolates per tick.
  - realtime_follower.py — target velocity now v_leader + ω×r_offset_rotated
  instead of just v_leader; leader heading is also extrapolated by
  ω·Δt_since_packet when packets are late.

  Option C is validated and stable. Ready to proceed to Option B
  (MAVSDK-in-the-loop) whenever you are — the architecture will carry over
  cleanly since the wire protocol + follower's target-computation math are
  already proven.

● Before I write code — one thing to set expectations on. Option B is the
  centralized MAVSDK controller I described earlier: one process owning MAVSDK
  connections to every drone, subscribing to the leader's telemetry, computing
  follower targets, and pushing set_position_velocity_ned setpoints. The math
  you already validated in Option C carries straight over; what's new is the
  MAVSDK plumbing plus pre-flight/takeoff/land lifecycle.

  rotation math + heading-rate feed-forward from Option C, and keeps the

   with the visualizer).

  To actually fly it (SITL path)

  # 1. Build PX4 (one-time, ~20 min). The repo's directory is already cloned.
  cd multiple_sitl/PX4-Autopilot
  make px4_sitl_default

  # 2. Launch N SITL instances (use the existing helper).
  cd ../.. ; ./multiple_sitl/multiple_sitl.sh -n 5 -m iris

  # 3. In a separate terminal per drone, start a mavsdk_server.
  # Port convention: hw_id N → gRPC port 50040+N, MAVLink UDP 14540+N.
  ./mavsdk_server -p 50041 udp://:14541 &   # drone 1 (leader)
  ./mavsdk_server -p 50042 udp://:14542 &   # drone 2
  ./mavsdk_server -p 50043 udp://:14543 &
  ./mavsdk_server -p 50044 udp://:14544 &
  ./mavsdk_server -p 50045 udp://:14545 &

  # 4. Dry-run first — connects, reads telemetry, computes setpoints, WITHOUT
  #    arming or offboard. This validates the whole stack short of motion.
  cd Simple_swarm
  python3 realtime_swarm_mavsdk.py \
      --config      test_inputs/01_basic_spear/drones_config.json \
      --formation   test_inputs/01_basic_spear/formation.csv \
      --leader-source csv \
      --leader-path test_inputs/01_basic_spear/leader_path.csv \
      --duration 15 \
      --dry-run

  # 5. Full run — arms, takes off, flies the formation, lands.
  python3 realtime_swarm_mavsdk.py \
      --config      test_inputs/01_basic_spear/drones_config.json \
      --formation   test_inputs/01_basic_spear/formation.csv \
      --leader-source csv \
      --leader-path test_inputs/01_basic_spear/leader_path.csv \
      --takeoff-alt 10 \
      --control-rate 20 \
      --duration 15 \
      --land-on-exit

  # 6. Visualize what actually flew.
  python3 visualize_trajectories.py \
      --trajectories-dir output/mavsdk_realtime \
      --save-dir output/mavsdk_realtime --no-show

  Design choices worth knowing

  - Centralized topology (that's what Option B means): one Python process owns
  all MAVSDK connections. Simpler than Option C's distributed processes, and
  leader+follower state lives in one address space so there's no protocol — no
  UDP packets on this path.
  - Leader source is pluggable: --leader-source mavsdk subscribes to drone 1's
  position_velocity_ned() + attitude_euler(); --leader-source csv replays a path
   file (useful for benching followers when you don't have a leader drone, or
  when the leader is flown manually via QGC and you want to simulate it first).
  - Frame assumption: everyone uses the same NED origin. In SITL this holds when
   you spawn all drones at the same XY. For real flight across distances you'll
  want to switch to global (lat/lon) broadcasts and do LLA→local NED per
  follower — that's a future patch, matches what
  src/drone_config.py:convert_LLA_to_NED does.
  - Setpoint set_position_velocity_ned (not set_position_ned): we push position
  and the velocity feed-forward (including the rotational term ω × r that the
  Option C test procedure flushed out). PX4 blends both, so tracking on rotating
   leaders stays tight.
  - No onboard saturation or PD: PX4 already has a position controller with its
  own limits. We just send references and let the flight controller do the
  closed-loop work. The PD-with-saturation code from Option C is gone in Option
  B.
  - Dry-run is the safest first test: connects, reads telemetry, computes
  setpoints, logs — never arms, never goes offboard. Use it to confirm wiring
  before enabling motion.

  Known gap

  Full end-to-end validation against a flying (or SITL-flying) drone wasn't
  possible in this environment. The code path has been exercised as far as we
  can without a mavsdk_server responding. When you're ready to test with SITL,
  run the steps above — we've validated the rotation math and heading-rate
  feed-forward in Option C already, so if connection + offboard engage
  successfully, tracking performance should be comparable (PX4's onboard
  position controller is tighter than our kinematic PD, so expect better not
  worse).

      10 +   processes talk over localhost UDP. Each follower runs its ow
         +n kinematic PD                                                 
      11 +   tracker. Validates the protocol, rates, and follower math be
         +fore touching                                                  
      12 +   an autopilot.                                               
      13 +3. **Option B — real-time, MAVSDK-in-the-loop** — a single proc
         +ess owns                                                       
      14 +   MAVSDK connections to every drone (or SITL instance), subscr
         +ibes to the                                                    
      15 +   leader's telemetry, computes setpoints, and pushes them via 
         +offboard mode.                                                 
      16  
      17 +The offline tier is self-contained (no MAVSDK, no sockets). Opt
         +ion C uses                                                     
      18 +loopback UDP only. Option B talks to `mavsdk_server` instances 
         +connected to                                                   
      19 +PX4. Every tier shares the same rotation math (§5.2) and the sa
         +me output CSV                                                  
      20 +schema (§5.5), so the visualizer / animator work on any stage's
         + logs.                                                         
      21 +                                                               
      22  ---

  ⎿  Added 194 lines, removed 1 line
      397  
      398  ---
      399  
      400 -## 7. Visualization                                           
      400 +## 7. Real-time tiers (Option C + Option B)                   
      401  
      402 +The offline planner produces CSVs that every timestep is pre-c
          +omputed. The                                                  
      403 +real-time tiers do the same math **per tick in wall-clock time
          +**: the leader                                                
      404 +state becomes a live signal (either replayed from CSV or read 
          +off MAVSDK                                                    
      405 +telemetry), and each follower consumes it through a fixed-rate
          + control                                                      
      406 +loop.                                                         
      407 +                                                              
      408 +### 7.1 Wire protocol (Option C only)                         
      409 +                                                              
      410 +One UDP JSON datagram per leader tick to each follower, define
          +d in                                                          
      411 +`realtime_protocol.py`:                                       
      412 +                                                              
      413 +```json                                                       
      414 +{                                                             
      415 +  "seq": 42, "hw_id": 1, "t_leader": 4.20,                    
      416 +  "n":  8.4, "e":  1.8, "alt": 10.84,                         
      417 +  "heading_deg": 21.0,                                        
      418 +  "vn": 2.0, "ve": 0.42, "vd": -0.2,                          
      419 +  "heading_rate_dps": 15.0                                    
      420 +}                                                             
      421 +```                                                           
      422 +                                                              
      423 +- `n, e, alt` — leader world-NED position (altitude positive u
          +p).                                                           
      424 +- `vn, ve, vd` — leader world-NED velocity (`vd > 0` = descend
          +ing).                                                         
      425 +- `heading_deg` — continuous (unwrapped) leader heading in deg
          +rees.                                                         
      426 +- `heading_rate_dps` — dθ/dt in deg/s. **Critical for rotating
          + formations**                                                 
      427 +  (see §7.4). Followers that see a 0 here behave correctly for
          + straight-line                                                
      428 +  translation; they lag badly when the leader turns in place. 
      429 +                                                              
      430 +Port convention: follower hw_id `N` binds to `127.0.0.1:37540 
          ++ N`. The                                                     
      431 +base is centralized in `REALTIME_PORT_BASE` and chosen to stay
          + clear of the                                                 
      432 +repo's MAVSDK (`14540+`) and GCS (`34550`) ranges.            
      433 +                                                              
      434 +Option B does not use this protocol — everything lives in one 
          +process, so                                                   
      435 +leader state is just shared Python state (`LeaderState` in    
      436 +`realtime_swarm_mavsdk.py`).                                  
      437 +                                                              
      438 +### 7.2 Option C architecture                                 
      439 +                                                              
      440 +Three process types:                                          
      441 +                                                              
      442 +| Process | Responsibilities |                                
      443 +|---|---|                                                     
      444 +| `realtime_leader.py` | Reads `leader_path.csv`, pre-computes
          + tangent heading and velocities, interpolates at wall-clock ti
          +me, unicasts a packet to every follower each tick, logs its ow
          +n trajectory. |                                               
      445 +| `realtime_follower.py` (one per drone) | Binds its UDP port 
          +non-blocking, waits for the first leader packet, initializes s
          +tate at `leader_pos + R(heading)·offset_body`, then runs a fix
          +ed-rate loop. |                                               
      446 +| `run_realtime_demo.py` | Spawns followers first (so they bin
          +d before the leader sends), then the leader, waits for all to 
          +finish, optionally runs the visualizer. |                     
      447 +                                                              
      448 +**Rate separation**: the leader broadcasts at `--rate` Hz (def
          +ault 10) and                                                  
      449 +each follower runs its inner control loop at `--sim-rate` Hz (
          +default 50).                                                  
      450 +Between leader packets the follower **linearly extrapolates** 
          +using the last                                                
      451 +received velocity and heading rate:                           
      452 +                                                              
      453 +```                                                           
      454 +Δt = now - last_packet_rx_time                                
      455 +leader_n(now)       = last.n        + last.vn * Δt            
      456 +leader_e(now)       = last.e        + last.ve * Δt            
      457 +leader_alt(now)     = last.alt      + (-last.vd) * Δt         
          + # NED sign                                                   
      458 +leader_heading(now) = last.heading  + last.heading_rate_dps * 
          +Δt                                                            
      459 +```                                                           
      460 +                                                              
      461 +This works because the inner loop runs at 50 Hz while packets 
          +arrive at 10                                                  
      462 +Hz — followers see a prediction-between-updates that is always
          + within one                                                   
      463 +leader period of ground truth. At 10 Hz on loopback, typical p
          +acket jitter                                                  
      464 +was <1 ms in our test runs.                                   
      465 +                                                              
      466 +**Inner control loop** (per sim tick, see §6.2–6.4 — it's the 
          +offline                                                       
      467 +simulator's PD tracker with saturation, reused verbatim):     
      468 +                                                              
      469 +```python                                                     
      470 +rot_n, rot_e = R(leader_heading) · (offset_n, offset_e)       
      471 +ω = radians(leader_heading_rate_dps)                          
      472 +                                                              
      473 +target_pos = (leader_n + rot_n,                               
      474 +              leader_e + rot_e,                               
      475 +              -(leader_alt + offset_alt))            # NED dow
          +n positive                                                    
      476 +target_vel = (leader_vn - ω·rot_e,                   # §7.4 fe
          +ed-forward                                                    
      477 +              leader_ve + ω·rot_n,                            
      478 +              leader_vd)                                      
      479 +target_yaw = radians(leader_heading)                          
      480 +                                                              
      481 +# PD + saturation (same as simulate_swarm.py DEFAULT_LIMITS)  
      482 +acc = kp*(target_pos - pos) + kv*(target_vel - vel)           
      483 +# clamp acc / vel / yaw_rate, Euler integrate                 
      484 +```                                                           
      485 +                                                              
      486 +**Shutdown**: each follower exits after `--silence-timeout` se
          +conds with no                                                 
      487 +leader packet (default 2 s). In real flight you'd replace this
          + with a                                                       
      488 +"hover in place" safety; for this demo, exit is fine.         
      489 +                                                              
      490 +### 7.3 Option B architecture                                 
      491 +                                                              
      492 +One process, no sockets on the control path:                  
      493 +                                                              
      494 +```                                                           
      495 +realtime_swarm_mavsdk.py (async main)                         
      496 +├── connect_drone() for every hw_id in config   → System objec
          +ts on 127.0.0.1:50040+hw_id                                   
      497 +├── wait_gps_ok()                                 (skipped in 
          +--dry-run)                                                    
      498 +├── arm_takeoff() on every drone                  (skipped in 
          +--dry-run)                                                    
      499 +├── start_offboard_at_current() on each follower  (skipped in 
          +--dry-run)                                                    
      500 +├── leader_{mavsdk,csv}_*_task() → LeaderState    (pick via --
          +leader-source)                                                
      501 +├── control_loop()  @ --control-rate Hz:                      
      502 +│     target = rotation + feed-forward (§7.4)                 
      503 +│     drone.offboard.set_position_velocity_ned(...)  per follo
          +wer                                                           
      504 +├── telemetry_logger_task() per drone → achieved CSV          
      505 +└── graceful_shutdown(): stop offboard, optional land         
      506 +```                                                           
      507 +                                                              
      508 +**Leader state source** is pluggable:                         
      509 +                                                              
      510 +- `--leader-source mavsdk` — subscribes to the leader drone's 
      511 +  `telemetry.position_velocity_ned()` and `telemetry.attitude_
          +euler()`                                                      
      512 +  streams. Heading rate is computed in-process by shortest-ang
          +le finite                                                     
      513 +  difference on consecutive yaw samples                       
      514 +  (`LeaderState.update_heading` — handles 359°→1° correctly as
          + +2°, not                                                     
      515 +  −358°).                                                     
      516 +- `--leader-source csv` — replays `leader_path.csv` in wall-cl
          +ock time                                                      
      517 +  into `LeaderState` at 50 Hz (same math as `realtime_leader.p
          +y`, but                                                       
      518 +  in-process).                                                
      519 +                                                              
      520 +**Setpoint** is pushed via                                    
      521 +`drone.offboard.set_position_velocity_ned(PositionNedYaw(...),
      522 +VelocityNedYaw(...))`. Position *and* velocity — PX4's onboard
          + position                                                     
      523 +controller blends both, so the rotational feed-forward (§7.4) 
          +is essential                                                  
      524 +for rotating formations.                                      
      525 +                                                              
      526 +**No PD / saturation in Option B** — PX4 has its own position 
          +controller                                                    
      527 +with its own limits. We send references; the flight controller
          + closes the                                                   
      528 +loop. That means no `kp/kv/kyaw`, no accel/velocity clamps in 
          +this script.                                                  
      529 +                                                              
      530 +**`--dry-run`**: still connects, still reads telemetry, still 
          +computes                                                      
      531 +setpoints, still logs — but never calls `arm()`, `takeoff()`, 
          +or                                                            
      532 +`offboard.start()`. Use it to validate wiring without putting 
          +vehicles in                                                   
      533 +motion.                                                       
      534 +                                                              
      535 +**Frame assumption**: everyone shares one NED origin. In SITL 
          +this holds                                                    
      536 +when you spawn all drones at the same XY. For real flight acro
          +ss distances                                                  
      537 +you'll want to switch to global (lat/lon) broadcasts and do   
      538 +`LLA → local NED` per follower, like `src/drone_config.py::con
          +vert_LLA_to_NED`                                              
      539 +does. Not wired up yet.                                       
      540 +                                                              
      541 +### 7.4 Rotational velocity feed-forward                      
      542 +                                                              
      543 +When the leader rotates, each follower's world-NED position ch
          +anges even                                                    
      544 +if the leader is stationary (e.g. a hover-and-rotate). The off
          +line planner                                                  
      545 +captures this automatically because `np.gradient` on the comma
          +nded                                                          
      546 +trajectory bakes the tangential velocity into the output CSV. 
          +The                                                           
      547 +real-time tiers must reconstruct it.                          
      548 +                                                              
      549 +Derivation: with body offset `(oₙ, o_e)` rotated by leader hea
          +ding θ(t):                                                    
      550 +                                                              
      551 +```                                                           
      552 +rot_n(t) = oₙ·cos θ(t) − o_e·sin θ(t)                         
      553 +rot_e(t) = oₙ·sin θ(t) + o_e·cos θ(t)                         
      554 +                                                              
      555 +d(rot_n)/dt = θ̇·(−oₙ·sin θ − o_e·cos θ) = −θ̇·rot_e            
      556 +d(rot_e)/dt = θ̇·( oₙ·cos θ − o_e·sin θ) = +θ̇·rot_n            
      557 +```                                                           
      558 +                                                              
      559 +So the follower's total target velocity is:                   
      560 +                                                              
      561 +```                                                           
      562 +target_vn = leader_vn − ω·rot_e                               
      563 +target_ve = leader_ve + ω·rot_n                               
      564 +target_vd = leader_vd                                         
      565 +```                                                           
      566 +                                                              
      567 +where `ω = radians(heading_rate_dps)`. This was the bug the re
          +al-time test                                                  
      568 +suite caught in `05_hover_rotate`: without it, peak tracking e
          +rror was                                                      
      569 +**1.64 m** because the PD controller had only position feedbac
          +k for a                                                       
      570 +follower orbiting a stationary leader at 2.1 m/s. With the fee
          +d-forward it                                                  
      571 +drops to **0.30 m** — a 5× improvement. Applied in both Option
          + C                                                            
      572 +(`realtime_follower.py`) and Option B (`control_loop` in      
      573 +`realtime_swarm_mavsdk.py`).                                  
      574 +                                                              
      575 +### 7.5 What Option C uses from the offline simulator         
      576 +                                                              
      577 +The per-follower inner loop in Option C is literally a copy of
          + the offline                                                  
      578 +PD + saturation kinematic model from §6. So:                  
      579 +                                                              
      580 +- Option C output CSVs have the same schema as simulated ones 
          +(§5.5), and                                                   
      581 +  `visualize_trajectories.py` / `animate_trajectories.py` work
          + unchanged.                                                   
      582 +- The tracking bounds in `run_realtime_tests.py` (§9.2) compar
          +e Option C                                                    
      583 +  output against the **offline** planner's output for the same
          + scenario,                                                    
      584 +  since the offline plan is the ground-truth target the real-t
          +ime                                                           
      585 +  integration should reproduce.                               
      586 +                                                              
      587 +Option B output CSVs use the same schema but the `px, py, pz, 
          +vx, vy, vz`                                                   
      588 +columns come from MAVSDK telemetry (what PX4 actually flew), n
          +ot from an                                                    
      589 +integrator — so they can include sensor noise, vehicle dynamic
          +s, and wind.                                                  
      590 +

      630 -## 8. Test procedure                                          
      630 +## 9. Test procedure                                          
      631  
      632 -### 8.1 Scenarios                                             
      632 +### 9.1 Scenarios                                             
      633  
      634  | # | Folder | Fleet | Leader motion | What it tests |
      635  |---|---|---|---|---|
     ...
      639  | 4 | `04_infeasible_tight_limits` | 2-follower V | 20 m/s lea
           der, 45°/s yaw | simulator's safety-threshold exit code (expec
           ted fail) |
      640  | 5 | `05_hover_rotate` | 7 followers on a 4 m ring | hover + 
           30°/s yaw | rigid-body rotation around stationary leader |
      641  
      642 -### 8.2 Running                                               
      642 +### 9.2 Two independent runners                               
      643  
      644 +**Offline runner** — `run_tests.py`. Runs all 5 scenarios thro
          +ugh                                                           
      645 +`generate_trajectories.py → simulate_swarm.py`. Parses the sim
          +ulator's                                                      
      646 +stdout report and compares against `meta.json` bounds. Synchro
          +nous, ~2 s                                                    
      647 +total wall-clock. Used as the regression gate for planner and 
          +simulator                                                     
      648 +logic.                                                        
      649 +                                                              
      650 +**Real-time runner** — `run_realtime_tests.py`. Runs all scena
          +rios *except*                                                 
      651 +`04_infeasible_tight_limits` (not meaningful at the Option C l
          +evel — the                                                    
      652 +simulator's safety test is about clamp behavior, not real-time
          + protocol)                                                    
      653 +through `run_realtime_demo.py`. For every scenario:           
      654 +                                                              
      655 +1. Ensures the offline ground truth exists (generates it if mi
          +ssing).                                                       
      656 +2. Spawns leader + all followers, waits for natural exit.     
      657 +3. Verifies orchestrator + subprocess exit codes are 0.       
      658 +4. Checks leader packet count is within ±10% of `rate × durati
          +on`.                                                          
      659 +5. Checks leader and worst-follower tick p95 jitter < 30 ms.  
      660 +6. Compares every follower's achieved CSV against the offline 
          +ground                                                        
      661 +   truth, skipping the first 1 s to let the PD transient settl
          +e. Peak                                                       
      662 +   3D tracking error must stay under a per-scenario tolerance 
      663 +   (`TRACKING_TOL_M` map in the runner).                      
      664 +                                                              
      665 +Real-time runner takes ~85 s (runs in wall-clock) and is CI-re
          +ady — exit                                                    
      666 +code 1 on any failure. When we introduced the heading-rate fee
          +d-forward                                                     
      667 +(§7.4), it was the hover-rotate scenario in *this* runner that
          + failed                                                       
      668 +first and drove the fix. Numbers after the fix:               
      669 +                                                              
      670 +| scenario | before | after | tolerance |                     
      671 +|---|---|---|---|                                             
      672 +| 01_basic_spear         | 0.31 m | 0.06 m | 0.60 m |         
      673 +| 02_circle_auto_heading | 0.47 m | 0.22 m | 0.60 m |         
      674 +| 03_vertical_spiral     | 0.89 m | 0.46 m | 1.00 m |         
      675 +| 05_hover_rotate        | 1.64 m ✗ | 0.30 m | 0.80 m |       
      676 +                                                              
      677 +Option B (MAVSDK) doesn't have a built-in test runner — it nee
          +ds a live                                                     
      678 +`mavsdk_server` connected to PX4 (SITL or real), and the code-
          +level checks                                                  
      679 +it would run are already covered by the Option C runner plus n
          +on-MAVSDK                                                     
      680 +unit checks inside `realtime_swarm_mavsdk.py` (config loading,
          + rotation                                                     
      681 +math, CSV leader replay, shortest-angle heading rate). End-to-
          +end Option B                                                  
      682 +validation is done against the SITL setup documented in §12.  
      683 +                                                              
      684 +### 9.3 Running                                               
      685 +                                                              
      686  ```bash
      645 -python3 _gen_test_inputs.py          # one-time, regenerates t
          -est_inputs/                                                   
      646 -python3 run_tests.py                 # runs the full pipeline 
          -for every scenario                                            
      647 -python3 run_tests.py --filter spiral # substring filter on fol
          -der name                                                      
      648 -python3 run_tests.py -v              # verbose output paths   
      687 +# Offline regression suite (fast, ~2 s)                       
      688 +python3 _gen_test_inputs.py            # one-time, regenerates
          + test_inputs/                                                 
      689 +python3 run_tests.py                   # all five scenarios   
      690 +python3 run_tests.py --filter spiral                          
      691 +python3 run_tests.py -v                                       
      692 +                                                              
      693 +# Real-time regression suite (wall-clock, ~85 s for 4 scenario
          +s)                                                            
      694 +python3 run_realtime_tests.py                                 
      695 +python3 run_realtime_tests.py --filter hover                  
      696  ```
      697  
      651 -Exit code 0 = all scenarios matched expectations, 1 otherwise.
      698 +Both runners exit non-zero on any scenario failure.           
      699  
      653 -### 8.3 `meta.json` expectation schema                        
      700 +### 9.4 `meta.json` expectation schema                        
      701  
      702  ```json
      703  {
     ...
      710  }
      711  ```
      712  
      666 -The runner parses `simulate_swarm.py`'s stdout (regex over the
          - report                                                       
      667 -block) and compares against these bounds. Tighten a bound and 
          -a scenario                                                    
      668 -flips to `FAIL` — that's the regression-detection mechanism.  
      713 +The offline runner parses `simulate_swarm.py`'s stdout (regex 
          +over the                                                      
      714 +report block) and compares against these bounds. The real-time
          + runner uses                                                  
      715 +its own `TRACKING_TOL_M` table keyed by scenario name. Tighten
          + either bound                                                 
      716 +to convert a scenario into a sharper regression check.        
      717  
      670 -### 8.4 Adding a scenario

      722     `meta.json` (plus `limits.json` if custom).

      676 -3. Run `python3 _gen_test_inputs.py && python3 run_tests.py`.
